Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just gotten completely… unmoored, I guess. Yes—yes, I suppose I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. For weeks I felt almost… brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was sort of trivial, like people were making things harder than they needed to be.

Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. I don’t… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.

I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into a regional powerhouse, as though my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.

And at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.

[
  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.2, F31.9, F30.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just gotten completely… unmoored, I guess. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be up, making plans, writing things down, feeling like I was ready to go do something. I used to do that back in graduate school sometimes, you know, stay up with a yellow pad and think I’d solved everything by morning—but this feels different.\n\nNow it’s more that I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: During those nights when you slept only two or three hours and felt ready to go, did you also feel unusually high or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes—yes, I suppose I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. For weeks I felt almost… brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was sort of trivial, like people were making things harder than they needed to be.\n\nAnd then if anyone questioned me—my wife, an old colleague, even someone on the phone—I’d snap at them. I’d think, “Why are you getting in the way?” It felt like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible person in the room, which, at the time, I was quite sure was me.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel more powerful, talented, or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… at the time I wouldn’t have called it that, but yes. I really believed I could fix my retirement accounts myself, advise three different start-ups—people I barely had any business advising, honestly—and somehow turn the arts council into this big regional force.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it now. But then it felt obvious, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. Like if people would just listen to me, everything would work.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I remember people being worried, and I remember thinking they were overreacting, but as for being hospitalized because of it… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no in my head right this second.\n\nHospitals always make me think of when my father was ill, so I sort of tense up around the word. But for that period specifically, I’d have to think it through.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did your behavior cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I didn’t see it that way at first—I thought everyone else was being dramatic—but yes, it caused real problems.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I used to be quite careful with that sort of thing. And she’s been staying close to me, almost watching over me, for safety. I don’t like putting it that way, but that’s what it is.\n\nAnd I’ve become unreliable. The museum, the arts council, even friends—I just stopped showing up the way I was supposed to. Or I’d say I was coming and then not manage it, or get pulled into some other idea. I used to be someone people could count on, so… yes, it’s been serious.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you have beliefs that others said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose they did. At the time I didn’t think they were unrealistic at all, which is the strange part. I really thought I could repair my retirement accounts myself, and then somehow advise three start-ups—people I barely knew, really—and make the arts council into this big regional powerhouse.\n\nI’d sit there with papers spread everywhere, like I was back in graduate school preparing some grand lecture, and it all felt connected in my mind. Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks—much more. Since all those plans fell apart, I’ve been… low, and ashamed, and I’ve pulled away from people.\n\nIt’s hard to put a neat start date on it. I keep thinking of when the phone stopped ringing as much, and then I stopped answering anyway, and there was this stack of papers on my desk I couldn’t even look at. But it’s been about nine months, I’d say, most days feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others said were clearly untrue when you were not feeling unusually high or unusually depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the ordinary stretches, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ve had strong opinions before. My wife would say I can be stubborn about museum things, committee decisions, all that sort of nonsense that somehow eats up whole afternoons. But beliefs that were clearly untrue… no, I don’t recall that happening when I wasn’t especially up or down.\n\nAt least, not that anyone pointed out to me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you ever hear or see things that other people said were not there?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.\n\nIt was more that I was so caught up in my papers and calls and ideas. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Increased_Activity\nmanifestation: \"I had this engine running inside me that made sitting quietly feel impossible, so I was up before sunrise calling people, rewriting proposals, and moving from one task to another without stopping.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.

I was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.

It reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.

I’d think, “Why are you getting in the way?” It felt like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible person in the room, which, at the time, I was quite sure was me.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel more powerful, talented, or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I remember people being worried, and I remember thinking they were overreacting, but as for being hospitalized because of it… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no in my head right this second.\n\nHospitals always make me think of when my father was ill, so I sort of tense up around the word. But for that period specifically, I’d have to think it through.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did your behavior cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I didn’t see it that way at first—I thought everyone else was being dramatic—but yes, it caused real problems.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I used to be quite careful with that sort of thing. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 69 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into a regional powerhouse, as though my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. And not just that—I thought I could advise these three start-ups, as if they’d been waiting for someone like me to come along and fix everything.

And the arts council—well, I’d been involved there for years, you know, and it’s mostly bake-sale energy and committee minutes, bless them—but I honestly believed I could turn it into a regional powerhouse. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.

Now it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.

I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. And not just that—I thought I could advise these three start-ups, as if they’d been waiting for someone like me to come along and fix everything.\n\nAnd the arts council—well, I’d been involved there for years, you know, and it’s mostly bake-sale energy and committee minutes, bless them—but I honestly believed I could turn it into a regional powerhouse. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.\n\nNow it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, at the time I was completely convinced. If someone showed me numbers or tried to explain why the retirement accounts couldn’t be fixed the way I thought, I’d just think they weren’t seeing it clearly.

Same with those start-ups and the arts council thing. I really believed I could step in and make all of it work, like I had this better judgment than everyone else. Which is funny now, because the arts council once spent forty minutes arguing about whether a flyer should be cream or off-white—but back then I thought, no, I’m the one who understands the bigger picture. I’d think, “Why are you getting in the way?” It felt like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible person in the room, which, at the time, I was quite sure was me.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel more powerful, talented, or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like if people would just listen to me, everything would work.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t remember anyone using the word “delusions,” exactly. People did say I was being unrealistic, that I was overestimating what I could do.

But I really did believe it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts somehow, advise those three start-ups, and turn the arts council into something much bigger—regional, important, you know. I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. Which is embarrassing to say now, because those arts council meetings could get bogged down in the smallest things, like who was bringing coffee, and I thought I was going to transform the whole thing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.2, F31.9, F30.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just gotten completely… unmoored, I guess. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be up, making plans, writing things down, feeling like I was ready to go do something. I used to do that back in graduate school sometimes, you know, stay up with a yellow pad and think I’d solved everything by morning—but this feels different.\n\nNow it’s more that I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: During those nights when you slept only two or three hours and felt ready to go, did you also feel unusually high or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes—yes, I suppose I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. For weeks I felt almost… brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was sort of trivial, like people were making things harder than they needed to be.\n\nAnd then if anyone questioned me—my wife, an old colleague, even someone on the phone—I’d snap at them. I’d think, “Why are you getting in the way?” It felt like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible person in the room, which, at the time, I was quite sure was me.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel more powerful, talented, or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… at the time I wouldn’t have called it that, but yes. I really believed I could fix my retirement accounts myself, advise three different start-ups—people I barely had any business advising, honestly—and somehow turn the arts council into this big regional force.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it now. But then it felt obvious, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. Like if people would just listen to me, everything would work.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I remember people being worried, and I remember thinking they were overreacting, but as for being hospitalized because of it… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no in my head right this second.\n\nHospitals always make me think of when my father was ill, so I sort of tense up around the word. But for that period specifically, I’d have to think it through.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did your behavior cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I didn’t see it that way at first—I thought everyone else was being dramatic—but yes, it caused real problems.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I used to be quite careful with that sort of thing. And she’s been staying close to me, almost watching over me, for safety. I don’t like putting it that way, but that’s what it is.\n\nAnd I’ve become unreliable. The museum, the arts council, even friends—I just stopped showing up the way I was supposed to. Or I’d say I was coming and then not manage it, or get pulled into some other idea. I used to be someone people could count on, so… yes, it’s been serious.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you have beliefs that others said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose they did. At the time I didn’t think they were unrealistic at all, which is the strange part. I really thought I could repair my retirement accounts myself, and then somehow advise three start-ups—people I barely knew, really—and make the arts council into this big regional powerhouse.\n\nI’d sit there with papers spread everywhere, like I was back in graduate school preparing some grand lecture, and it all felt connected in my mind. Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks—much more. Since all those plans fell apart, I’ve been… low, and ashamed, and I’ve pulled away from people.\n\nIt’s hard to put a neat start date on it. I keep thinking of when the phone stopped ringing as much, and then I stopped answering anyway, and there was this stack of papers on my desk I couldn’t even look at. But it’s been about nine months, I’d say, most days feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others said were clearly untrue when you were not feeling unusually high or unusually depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the ordinary stretches, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ve had strong opinions before. My wife would say I can be stubborn about museum things, committee decisions, all that sort of nonsense that somehow eats up whole afternoons. But beliefs that were clearly untrue… no, I don’t recall that happening when I wasn’t especially up or down.\n\nAt least, not that anyone pointed out to me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you ever hear or see things that other people said were not there?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.\n\nIt was more that I was so caught up in my papers and calls and ideas. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nPatient: Yes. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. And not just that—I thought I could advise these three start-ups, as if they’d been waiting for someone like me to come along and fix everything.\n\nAnd the arts council—well, I’d been involved there for years, you know, and it’s mostly bake-sale energy and committee minutes, bless them—but I honestly believed I could turn it into a regional powerhouse. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.\n\nNow it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, at the time I was completely convinced. If someone showed me numbers or tried to explain why the retirement accounts couldn’t be fixed the way I thought, I’d just think they weren’t seeing it clearly.\n\nSame with those start-ups and the arts council thing. I really believed I could step in and make all of it work, like I had this better judgment than everyone else. Which is funny now, because the arts council once spent forty minutes arguing about whether a flyer should be cream or off-white—but back then I thought, no, I’m the one who understands the bigger picture. I didn’t take in the evidence. I just brushed it aside.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that those beliefs were delusions?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember anyone using the word “delusions,” exactly. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts somehow, advise those three start-ups, and turn the arts council into something much bigger—regional, important, you know. I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. I mean, I didn’t see it that way at first—I thought everyone else was being dramatic—but yes, it caused real problems.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I used to be quite careful with that sort of thing. And she’s been staying close to me, almost watching over me, for safety. I don’t like putting it that way, but that’s what it is.\n\nAnd I’ve become unreliable. The museum, the arts council, even friends—I just stopped showing up the way I was supposed to. Or I’d say I was coming and then not manage it, or get pulled into some other idea. Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep thinking of when the phone stopped ringing as much, and then I stopped answering anyway, and there was this stack of papers on my desk I couldn’t even look at. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.\n\nIt was more that I was so caught up in my papers and calls and ideas. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.\n\nNow it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, at the time I was completely convinced. If someone showed me numbers or tried to explain why the retirement accounts couldn’t be fixed the way I thought, I’d just think they weren’t seeing it clearly.\n\nSame with those start-ups and the arts council thing. I really believed I could step in and make all of it work, like I had this better judgment than everyone else. Which is funny now, because the arts council once spent forty minutes arguing about whether a flyer should be cream or off-white—but back then I thought, no, I’m the one who understands the bigger picture. I didn’t take in the evidence. I just brushed it aside.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that those beliefs were delusions?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember anyone using the word “delusions,” exactly. People did say I was being unrealistic, that I was overestimating what I could do.\n\nBut I really did believe it. Yes, I think so. Not dangerous like I was trying to hurt anyone, but clearly out of touch, yes.

I truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, like I’d somehow outthink the whole system, and I was convinced I could advise three start-ups even though—well, I’m 69, I’m not exactly the person people usually call for that sort of thing. It’s strange, because now I can see how inflated that was. At the time it felt completely reasonable, like everyone else was just moving too slowly or thinking too small.

And she’s been staying close to me, almost watching over me, for safety. I don’t like putting it that way, but that’s what it is.\n\nAnd I’ve become unreliable. The museum, the arts council, even friends—I just stopped showing up the way I was supposed to. Or I’d say I was coming and then not manage it, or get pulled into some other idea. I used to be someone people could count on, so… yes, it’s been serious.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you have beliefs that others said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose they did. At the time I didn’t think they were unrealistic at all, which is the strange part. I really thought I could repair my retirement accounts myself, and then somehow advise three start-ups—people I barely knew, really—and make the arts council into this big regional powerhouse.\n\nI’d sit there with papers spread everywhere, like I was back in graduate school preparing some grand lecture, and it all felt connected in my mind. Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks—much more. Since all those plans fell apart, I’ve been… low, and ashamed, and I’ve pulled away from people.\n\nIt’s hard to put a neat start date on it. I keep thinking of when the phone stopped ringing as much, and then I stopped answering anyway, and there was this stack of papers on my desk I couldn’t even look at. But it’s been about nine months, I’d say, most days feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others said were clearly untrue when you were not feeling unusually high or unusually depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the ordinary stretches, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ve had strong opinions before. My wife would say I can be stubborn about museum things, committee decisions, all that sort of nonsense that somehow eats up whole afternoons. But beliefs that were clearly untrue… no, I don’t recall that happening when I wasn’t especially up or down.\n\nAt least, not that anyone pointed out to me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you ever hear or see things that other people said were not there?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.\n\nIt was more that I was so caught up in my papers and calls and ideas. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. And not just that—I thought I could advise these three start-ups, as if they’d been waiting for someone like me to come along and fix everything.\n\nAnd the arts council—well, I’d been involved there for years, you know, and it’s mostly bake-sale energy and committee minutes, bless them—but I honestly believed I could turn it into a regional powerhouse. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.\n\nNow it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, at the time I was completely convinced. If someone showed me numbers or tried to explain why the retirement accounts couldn’t be fixed the way I thought, I’d just think they weren’t seeing it clearly.\n\nSame with those start-ups and the arts council thing. I really believed I could step in and make all of it work, like I had this better judgment than everyone else. Which is funny now, because the arts council once spent forty minutes arguing about whether a flyer should be cream or off-white—but back then I thought, no, I’m the one who understands the bigger picture. I didn’t take in the evidence. I just brushed it aside.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that those beliefs were delusions?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember anyone using the word “delusions,” exactly. People did say I was being unrealistic, that I was overestimating what I could do.\n\nBut I really did believe it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts somehow, advise those three start-ups, and turn the arts council into something much bigger—regional, important, you know. I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. Which is embarrassing to say now, because those arts council meetings could get bogged down in the smallest things, like who was bringing coffee, and I thought I was going to transform the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe with complete certainty that you had special powers or abilities beyond what is realistically possible?\n\nPatient: Special powers… I don’t know if I would’ve said it that way, like flying through the air or hearing voices from heaven or anything. But yes, I had complete certainty that I had abilities I really didn’t have.\n\nI truly believed I could fix my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly become smarter than all the people who’d been advising me. And I thought I could advise three start-ups—three, as if I’d been sitting around waiting for Silicon Valley to call, which is absurd now that I say it. And the arts council, I was sure I could turn it into this major regional force.\n\nSo… not magic powers, exactly. But I absolutely believed my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s, beyond what was realistic.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did those beliefs ever lead you to do something dangerous or clearly out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not dangerous like I was trying to hurt anyone, but clearly out of touch, yes.\n\nI truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, like I’d somehow outthink the whole system, and I was convinced I could advise three start-ups even though—well, I’m 69, I’m not exactly the person people usually call for that sort of thing. And the arts council… I really thought I could turn it into this regional powerhouse. I remember talking as if it was just obvious I had the judgment to do it.\n\nIt’s strange, because now I can see how inflated that was. At the time it felt completely reasonable, like everyone else was just moving too slowly or thinking too small.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People did say I was being unrealistic, that I was overestimating what I could do.\n\nBut I really did believe it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts somehow, advise those three start-ups, and turn the arts council into something much bigger—regional, important, you know. I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. Which is embarrassing to say now, because those arts council meetings could get bogged down in the smallest things, like who was bringing coffee, and I thought I was going to transform the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe with complete certainty that you had special powers or abilities beyond what is realistically possible?\n\nPatient: Special powers… I don’t know if I would’ve said it that way, like flying through the air or hearing voices from heaven or anything. But yes, I had complete certainty that I had abilities I really didn’t have.\n\nI truly believed I could fix my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly become smarter than all the people who’d been advising me. And I thought I could advise three start-ups—three, as if I’d been sitting around waiting for Silicon Valley to call, which is absurd now that I say it. But I absolutely believed my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s, beyond what was realistic.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did those beliefs ever lead you to do something dangerous or clearly out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, there were times like that. I believed I could do things other people thought were just not realistic—repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, turn the arts council into some big regional powerhouse.

And I know how that sounds now. The arts council especially—half the time we were talking about budgets or who knew which donor, and I was sitting there thinking I could remake the whole thing. It felt obvious to me then, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. People would tell me, “That’s not how this works,” or “You can’t just do that,” and I remember thinking they were the ones who didn’t understand.

[

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.2, F31.9, F30.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly it’s the sleep. It’s just gotten completely… unmoored, I guess. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be up, making plans, writing things down, feeling like I was ready to go do something. I used to do that back in graduate school sometimes, you know, stay up with a yellow pad and think I’d solved everything by morning—but this feels different.\n\nNow it’s more that I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested at all. It’s been wearing me down.\n\nDoctor: During those nights when you slept only two or three hours and felt ready to go, did you also feel unusually high or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yes—yes, I suppose I did. It wasn’t just “happy,” exactly. For weeks I felt almost… brilliantly optimistic, like every obstacle was sort of trivial, like people were making things harder than they needed to be.\n\nAnd then if anyone questioned me—my wife, an old colleague, even someone on the phone—I’d snap at them. I’d think, “Why are you getting in the way?” It felt like they were trying to sabotage the only sensible person in the room, which, at the time, I was quite sure was me.\n\nDoctor: During that period, did you feel more powerful, talented, or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… at the time I wouldn’t have called it that, but yes. I really believed I could fix my retirement accounts myself, advise three different start-ups—people I barely had any business advising, honestly—and somehow turn the arts council into this big regional force.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous saying it now. But then it felt obvious, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. Like if people would just listen to me, everything would work.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you hospitalized because of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I remember people being worried, and I remember thinking they were overreacting, but as for being hospitalized because of it… I don’t have a clear yes-or-no in my head right this second.\n\nHospitals always make me think of when my father was ill, so I sort of tense up around the word. But for that period specifically, I’d have to think it through.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did your behavior cause serious problems in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I didn’t see it that way at first—I thought everyone else was being dramatic—but yes, it caused real problems.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I used to be quite careful with that sort of thing. And she’s been staying close to me, almost watching over me, for safety. I don’t like putting it that way, but that’s what it is.\n\nAnd I’ve become unreliable. The museum, the arts council, even friends—I just stopped showing up the way I was supposed to. Or I’d say I was coming and then not manage it, or get pulled into some other idea. I used to be someone people could count on, so… yes, it’s been serious.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you have beliefs that others said were clearly untrue or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose they did. At the time I didn’t think they were unrealistic at all, which is the strange part. I really thought I could repair my retirement accounts myself, and then somehow advise three start-ups—people I barely knew, really—and make the arts council into this big regional powerhouse.\n\nI’d sit there with papers spread everywhere, like I was back in graduate school preparing some grand lecture, and it all felt connected in my mind. Like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s, and if people would just stop interrupting me and let me explain it, they’d see it too.\n\nNow, looking back… yes, other people were right to say it wasn’t realistic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks—much more. Since all those plans fell apart, I’ve been… low, and ashamed, and I’ve pulled away from people.\n\nIt’s hard to put a neat start date on it. I keep thinking of when the phone stopped ringing as much, and then I stopped answering anyway, and there was this stack of papers on my desk I couldn’t even look at. But it’s been about nine months, I’d say, most days feeling like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had beliefs that others said were clearly untrue when you were not feeling unusually high or unusually depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the ordinary stretches, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ve had strong opinions before. My wife would say I can be stubborn about museum things, committee decisions, all that sort of nonsense that somehow eats up whole afternoons. But beliefs that were clearly untrue… no, I don’t recall that happening when I wasn’t especially up or down.\n\nAt least, not that anyone pointed out to me.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you ever hear or see things that other people said were not there?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. Hearing or seeing things that weren’t there—no, I don’t remember anything like a voice in the room or seeing a person who wasn’t there.\n\nIt was more that I was so caught up in my papers and calls and ideas. I’d misplace things constantly, and then accuse the house of somehow swallowing them, which my wife used to say about my father’s old workshop—tools everywhere, not a single screwdriver where it belonged. But that’s not the same as seeing something, is it?\n\nSo… no, not that I noticed.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that your thinking had become bizarre or out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes… people did say that, or something close to it. Not “bizarre” exactly, maybe they used gentler words—my wife certainly did. She said I was acting as if I’d suddenly become the smartest man in every room.\n\nAnd at the time I truly believed it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, and turn the arts council into this whole regional powerhouse. Like my judgment was better than everyone else’s, and they just couldn’t see it yet. Which is embarrassing to say now, because I’ve sat through enough arts council meetings to know they can barely agree on where to put the coffee urn.\n\nDoctor: Before this long depressed period began, had you ever had a high-energy period like that without becoming depressed afterward?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so—at least there was that period before everything sort of… collapsed downward.\n\nI was just constantly moving. It felt like I had an engine running inside me, you know? Sitting quietly felt almost impossible. I’d be up before sunrise calling people—too early, probably, though at the time I thought everyone should be as ready as I was. I was rewriting proposals, starting one thing, then another, then another.\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I used to help organize exhibits—there’s always a point where everyone is fussing over labels and lighting, and I’d think, “If people would just get out of the way, this could be done by lunch.” But this was more than that. I couldn’t seem to stop moving from task to task.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you ever convinced of something that other people said was impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was convinced, absolutely convinced, that I could straighten out my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly discovered the one angle the advisors had all missed. And not just that—I thought I could advise these three start-ups, as if they’d been waiting for someone like me to come along and fix everything.\n\nAnd the arts council—well, I’d been involved there for years, you know, and it’s mostly bake-sale energy and committee minutes, bless them—but I honestly believed I could turn it into a regional powerhouse. Like my judgment had become… superior, somehow. Everyone else was being slow or small-minded.\n\nNow it sounds ridiculous. At the time, though, it felt completely true.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were you completely convinced these ideas were true even when other people showed you clear evidence they were not?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, at the time I was completely convinced. If someone showed me numbers or tried to explain why the retirement accounts couldn’t be fixed the way I thought, I’d just think they weren’t seeing it clearly.\n\nSame with those start-ups and the arts council thing. I really believed I could step in and make all of it work, like I had this better judgment than everyone else. Which is funny now, because the arts council once spent forty minutes arguing about whether a flyer should be cream or off-white—but back then I thought, no, I’m the one who understands the bigger picture. I didn’t take in the evidence. I just brushed it aside.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did anyone tell you that those beliefs were delusions?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember anyone using the word “delusions,” exactly. People did say I was being unrealistic, that I was overestimating what I could do.\n\nBut I really did believe it. I thought I could fix my retirement accounts somehow, advise those three start-ups, and turn the arts council into something much bigger—regional, important, you know. I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. Which is embarrassing to say now, because those arts council meetings could get bogged down in the smallest things, like who was bringing coffee, and I thought I was going to transform the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe with complete certainty that you had special powers or abilities beyond what is realistically possible?\n\nPatient: Special powers… I don’t know if I would’ve said it that way, like flying through the air or hearing voices from heaven or anything. But yes, I had complete certainty that I had abilities I really didn’t have.\n\nI truly believed I could fix my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly become smarter than all the people who’d been advising me. And I thought I could advise three start-ups—three, as if I’d been sitting around waiting for Silicon Valley to call, which is absurd now that I say it. And the arts council, I was sure I could turn it into this major regional force.\n\nSo… not magic powers, exactly. But I absolutely believed my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s, beyond what was realistic.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did those beliefs ever lead you to do something dangerous or clearly out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not dangerous like I was trying to hurt anyone, but clearly out of touch, yes.\n\nI truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, like I’d somehow outthink the whole system, and I was convinced I could advise three start-ups even though—well, I’m 69, I’m not exactly the person people usually call for that sort of thing. And the arts council… I really thought I could turn it into this regional powerhouse. I remember talking as if it was just obvious I had the judgment to do it.\n\nIt’s strange, because now I can see how inflated that was. At the time it felt completely reasonable, like everyone else was just moving too slowly or thinking too small.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were there times when you believed things that other people said were impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes, there were times like that. I believed I could do things other people thought were just not realistic—repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, turn the arts council into some big regional powerhouse.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. The arts council especially—half the time we were talking about budgets or who knew which donor, and I was sitting there thinking I could remake the whole thing. It felt obvious to me then, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. People would tell me, “That’s not how this works,” or “You can’t just do that,” and I remember thinking they were the ones who didn’t understand.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates":["F31.2"],"note":"Grandiose delusions occurred only during a clear manic episode, with subsequent major depressive episode and no psychosis outside mood episodes; this rules out F30.2 (single manic episode) and makes unspecified bipolar disorder unnecessary versus bipolar I disorder, current/recent manic with psychotic features.","is_final":true}

I felt like my judgment had suddenly become better than everyone else’s. Which is embarrassing to say now, because those arts council meetings could get bogged down in the smallest things, like who was bringing coffee, and I thought I was going to transform the whole thing.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did you believe with complete certainty that you had special powers or abilities beyond what is realistically possible?\n\nPatient: Special powers… I don’t know if I would’ve said it that way, like flying through the air or hearing voices from heaven or anything. But yes, I had complete certainty that I had abilities I really didn’t have.\n\nI truly believed I could fix my retirement accounts, like I’d suddenly become smarter than all the people who’d been advising me. And I thought I could advise three start-ups—three, as if I’d been sitting around waiting for Silicon Valley to call, which is absurd now that I say it. And the arts council, I was sure I could turn it into this major regional force.\n\nSo… not magic powers, exactly. But I absolutely believed my judgment was suddenly superior to everyone else’s, beyond what was realistic.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did those beliefs ever lead you to do something dangerous or clearly out of touch with reality?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not dangerous like I was trying to hurt anyone, but clearly out of touch, yes.\n\nI truly believed I could repair my retirement accounts, like I’d somehow outthink the whole system, and I was convinced I could advise three start-ups even though—well, I’m 69, I’m not exactly the person people usually call for that sort of thing. And the arts council… I really thought I could turn it into this regional powerhouse. I remember talking as if it was just obvious I had the judgment to do it.\n\nIt’s strange, because now I can see how inflated that was. At the time it felt completely reasonable, like everyone else was just moving too slowly or thinking too small.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, were there times when you believed things that other people said were impossible or definitely not true?\n\nPatient: Yes, there were times like that. I believed I could do things other people thought were just not realistic—repair my retirement accounts, advise three start-ups, turn the arts council into some big regional powerhouse.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. The arts council especially—half the time we were talking about budgets or who knew which donor, and I was sitting there thinking I could remake the whole thing. It felt obvious to me then, like my judgment had suddenly become sharper than everyone else’s. People would tell me, “That’s not how this works,” or “You can’t just do that,” and I remember thinking they were the ones who didn’t understand."
